We've filled Thrace with the Enez, the Sakaryah, and the Kızırmak, and many other streams of industrial waste. We've seen fish die on TV many times. We all know that in the last few years in particular, we've filled our beautiful country's bonds, its suburbs, its plateaus with concrete, we've looted their cities, we've dried their lakes, we've chased their meadows, we've chased their forests. We destroyed the great streams of the Black Sea, where we slaughtered the living and the trees. As a result of the humbling use of the ground waters, the waters went deep into the depths, and there were sinkholes in the plains of the cones. We poorened the lakes with extreme unconscious hunting. We've destroyed the existing species, disrupting the biological balance, leaving different species inside the lake ecosystem. Lake Bendir is an example of this, and the natural crayfish population is dwindling as a result of species such as silver fish brought to the lake, fresh water coliosis. Use the lake. The users, whom we approved, acted without knowledge with short-term rants and profit concerns, destroying biological life and water products in lakes. As for our seas, the wealth of fish on our shores is reduced year by year. It's fish season, there's no fish under 20 lira a kilo, and fishermen complain that they can't make a living. We hunted baby fish because they didn't have enough control. The fish were damaged by the tearing of the Misina nets at sea. We broke the fish's nest by fishing with trolls. Excessive and unconscious hunting, due to environmental negative factors, is rapidly reducing natural fish resources, and some species are even becoming endangered. In short, our seas have become poor in terms of water products. Another important thing, but what is not in the law, is the environment. For example, the hunt needed to be arranged on the edge of depth in order to preserve biodiversity. This means not only is there no hunting in the depths, which are very important for the breeding of fish, but also the protection of marine meadows and other breeding grounds that are vital to marine life. The 40-meter ban is a border for protecting these meadows. Throwing nets on these meadows is not only to destroy the fish there, but also to destroy the fish's future. That's our limit, unfortunately, 24 meters. This matter would also have to be made clear. Given all these debates, it is clear that such legislation is needed, but the question is whether law materials can achieve success in the production of sustainable water products. That's why I'm opposed to the offer. Turkish agriculture is losing blood every day. The farmer's debt is constantly increasing. The impact of dollars and euros in input costs is very high because our agricultural sector is dependent on imports. I don't need to mention animal imports, for example. We need to feed the imported animals and the people in our country. Animal production is food at the beginning of input costs. Milk makes up 60 per cent of cattle and 40 per cent of livestock into bait entries. We import about 50 percent of the raw food we use. And our ceremonies are getting smaller and smaller. This shrinking makes the farmer more dependent on dollars and euros. In the 1980s, the Mera were reduced from 20 million hectares to 14 million hectares today. The bottom of this fall is the development of mera fields and the use of them unconscious and uncontrollably outside agricultural activities. As a result, we must take urgent and serious measures regarding agriculture, and I salute the General Assembly.

Congressmans, I'm also going to talk about the species of invasive fish that have been increasing on the coast of Mersin in recent years and have been in great danger. Because of climate change, rising sea water temperature forces marine creatures to migrate. As a result of these migrations, the number of foreign species starting to live in the Mediterranean is up to 1,000, while the number of species on the coast of Turkey exceeds 500. About 450 of these species are invasive species that pose a direct threat. 74 percent of the foreign species reaching the Mediterranean are coming through the Suez Canal, where the intruders first visited Mersin. Fish such as bulwark, lionfish, poisonous jellyfish, manda-eye fish, devilfish, fish that are not supposed to be on the shores of our city under normal conditions, but are common in recent years. These species seriously damage marine ecosystems, biodiversity, infecting indigenous species with parasites and disease, and even endangering their generation. These invading species, which directly affect fishing and tourism, are also dangerous to our people. We have to admit, in the near future, we have a serious biosecurity problem ahead of us. Soon, our seas will have a biosecurity problem. If the lionfish in Edremit, among the invading species, can get caught in the nets of a fisherman, there is a great problem for our fishing here. The poison in their thorns breeds very quickly, causing a great danger, and it specifically targets the crustacean population with the baby fish. Unfortunately, the same species is stable with reports of destruction in the Indian Ocean, the Western Atlantic, and the Caribbean, while it is inevitable to damage our ecosystem in the future. The efforts to control lionfish in these areas must be carefully monitored, experience transfer and if necessary, co-work groups must be formed. Another invasive species, balloon fish, have seen a huge increase in the recent years along the coast of Mersin. The poison of this fish in the class of poisonous fish is 1,000 times more effective than cyanide, 1,200 times more effective than arsenic, and its antidote is not yet available. Two weeks ago in the Mediterranean, hundreds of puffer fish caught in fishermen's nets reveal the danger in our seas. Three days ago, the cats in Mersin died, eating balloon fish that washed ashore. Besides, we had a very sad incident in Anamur County last summer, and the blowfish that attacked our eight-year-old child floating in the sea caused our daughter to lose her finger. We, the Christians, are concerned about the new attacks. We're concerned that these species will also affect tourists who come to our city. We're concerned about the breakdown of ecosystems in our seas. Our government is responsible for dealing with these concerns. Our call to the government is to create a network of marine and coastal protected areas before it's too late, while the invasive species are yet to be contained. It is the government's duty to coordinate all seas, to apply the world's fighting methods to our own seas, and to develop plans to act against invading species. In addition to the health and sustainability of the marine ecosystem, it is vital to our biosecurity. I salute our High Council.

The Law on Water Products, which has been in effect since 1971, has made significant changes to be made to be harmonised with modern conditions, including technological developments, scientific, environmental, social issues, and the needs of the sector. By offering the law, our main goal is to protect our seas, the creatures living in our seas, and our honest fishermen who are fishing, to make the balance of protection and use more effective. By increasing our fish presence, we aim to increase our own internal consumption and exports. It is also very important that they grow in their natural habitat as they increase the production of fish. For this purpose, important studies have been carried out in recent years about keeping our seas clean. The project continues integrated from marine waste to wastewater management, from recycling of waste to immediate intervention, from the preservation of marine ecosystems to the preservation of coastal habitats, from the pollution in our waters to the removal of ship's waste. From 350 separate points in our 28 provinces, which are on the coast of our seas, the samples taken at the proper time are examined over 50 different parameters, and water quality is constantly monitored. The same works continue in most of our river basins and in our lakes. We are more than half the population that lives in our provinces, which are on the coast of our seas. The Marmara Region represents only 11 per cent of our country's facial measurement. In contrast, 28 percent of our population lives in this region. Nearly one fifth of our country's population lives in Istanbul, and our industry operates mainly in this region. In 1994, only 5 per cent of the wastewaters in Istanbul could be refined, the rest were left in the sea; 125 fish living in Marmara were dropped to 4. First, we cleaned up four and a half million tons of mud in the Golden Horn. We have gathered wastewaters and harvested them by building collectors on both sides of the Halic and Bosphorus, and on all the coasts of Istanbul. We built 88 wastewater treatment facilities in Istanbul. Nine of them are pre-decontamination, others are biological and advanced biological waste water treatment facilities. Today, 99 percent of the wastewaters in Istanbul are cleansed and our seas can be breached from anywhere. Currently, at least 42 species of fish live in the Haliç. Five wastewater treatment facilities have been built for industrial facilities, and one is being continued. The desalination of the water here will no longer be released to Ergene.
